Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Artem found a grasshopper. He brought it to his house and constructed a jumping area for him.

The area looks like a strip of cells 1 × n. Each cell contains the direction for the next jump and the length of that jump. Grasshopper starts in the first cell and follows the instructions written on the cells. Grasshopper stops immediately if it jumps out of the strip. Now Artem wants to find out if this will ever happen.

Input

The first line of the input contains a single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000) — length of the strip. 

Next line contains a string of length n which consists of characters "<" and ">" only, that provide the direction of the jump from the corresponding cell. Next line contains n integers di (1 ≤ di ≤ 109) — the length of the jump from the i-th cell.

Output

Print "INFINITE" (without quotes) if grasshopper will continue his jumps forever. Otherwise print "FINITE" (without quotes).

Examples

Input

2
&gt;&lt;
1 2


Output

FINITE


Input

3
&gt;&gt;&lt;
2 1 1


Output

INFINITE

Note

In the first sample grasshopper starts from the first cell and jumps to the right on the next cell. When he is in the second cell he needs to jump two cells left so he will jump out of the strip.

Second sample grasshopper path is 1 - 3 - 2 - 3 - 2 - 3 and so on. The path is infinite.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of `n` cells, each containing a direction (`<` or `>`) and a jump length
2. Grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0) and follows the instructions
3. It jumps according to the direction and length from the current cell
4. If it jumps outside the strip (index < 0 or index >= n), it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper will jump forever (INFINITE) or eventually exit (FINITE)

## Key Insights

1. The grasshopper will jump forever only if it enters a cycle of cells that it keeps visiting repeatedly
2. If it ever jumps outside the strip, the answer is FINITE
3. If it visits the same cell twice, it's in a cycle and will continue forever
4. Since there are only n cells, if we visit any cell twice, we must be in a cycle

## Algorithm Approach

1. Simulate the grasshopper's movement step by step
2. Keep track of visited cells
3. If we jump outside the strip (index < 0 or index >= n), return FINITE
4. If we visit a cell we've seen before, return INFINITE (cycle detected)
5. If we reach a dead end, return FINITE

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input: n, direction string, jump lengths
2. Initialize current position as 0 (first cell)
3. Use a set to track visited positions
4. While the current position is valid:
   - If we've been to this position before → INFINITE (cycle)
   - If jumping would go outside bounds → FINITE
   - Mark current position as visited
   - Calculate next position based on direction and jump length
5. Return result

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
current_pos = 0

while True:
    # Check if current position is valid (within bounds)
    if current_pos < 0 or current_pos >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # If we've visited this position before, we're in a cycle
    if current_pos in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(current_pos)
    
    # Determine next position based on direction
    if directions[current_pos] == '>':
        next_pos = current_pos + jumps[current_pos]
    else:  # directions[current_pos] == '<'
        next_pos = current_pos - jumps[current_pos]
    
    # Update current position
    current_pos = next_pos
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1:** n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]
- Start at position 0
- Position 0: direction '>' → jump 1 → next position = 0+1 = 1
- Position 1: direction '<' → jump 2 → next position = 1-2 = -1
- -1 is outside bounds → FINITE ✓

**Example 2:** n=3, directions=">> <", jumps=[2,1,1] 
- Start at position 0
- Position 0: direction '>' → jump 2 → next position = 0+2 = 2
- Position 2: direction '<' → jump 1 → next position = 2-1 = 1
- Position 1: direction '>' → jump 1 → next position = 1+1 = 2
- Position 2: already visited → INFINITE ✓

The solution correctly handles both cases.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of n cells, each containing a direction ('<' or '>') and a jump length
2. Grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0)
3. From each cell, it jumps according to the direction and length specified
4. If the jump takes it outside the strip (to index < 0 or >= n), it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper will jump forever (INFINITE) or eventually exit (FINITE)

## Key Insights

1. If the grasshopper ever jumps outside the strip, the answer is "FINITE"
2. If the grasshopper enters a cycle (visits the same sequence of cells repeatedly), the answer is "INFINITE"
3. Since there are only n cells, if the grasshopper visits any cell twice, it will enter a cycle
4. We need to track which cells we've visited to detect cycles

## Algorithm

1. Start at position 0
2. Keep a set of visited positions
3. For each position:
   - If we've visited this position before, we detected a cycle → "INFINITE"
   - If the jump takes us outside the valid range [0, n-1], return "FINITE"
   - Otherwise, mark this position as visited and continue to the next position
4. If we process all possible positions without finding a cycle or exiting, we return "FINITE"

## Step-by-step solution

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jump_lengths = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
current_pos = 0

while True:
    # If we've visited this position before, we have a cycle
    if current_pos in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(current_pos)
    
    # Get the direction and jump length from current cell
    direction = directions[current_pos]
    jump_length = jump_lengths[current_pos]
    
    # Calculate next position based on direction
    if direction == '>':
        next_pos = current_pos + jump_length
    else:  # direction == '<'
        next_pos = current_pos - jump_length
    
    # Check if next position is outside the strip
    if next_pos < 0 or next_pos >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # Move to next position
    current_pos = next_pos
```

## Verification with examples

**Example 1:**
- n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]
- Start at 0: direction '>' → jump 1 → next_pos = 0+1 = 1
- At 1: direction '<' → jump 2 → next_pos = 1-2 = -1 < 0 → FINITE ✓

**Example 2:**
- n=3, directions=">> <", jumps=[2,1,1]
- Start at 0: direction '>' → jump 2 → next_pos = 0+2 = 2
- At 2: direction '<' → jump 1 → next_pos = 2-1 = 1
- At 1: direction '>' → jump 1 → next_pos = 1+1 = 2
- At 2: we've already visited position 2 → INFINITE ✓

The solution correctly handles both cases.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of n cells, each containing a direction ('<' or '>') and a jump length
2. Grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0) and follows instructions
3. It jumps according to the direction and length from each cell
4. We need to determine if the grasshopper will:
   - Jump out of bounds (FINITE)
   - Continue jumping forever in a cycle (INFINITE)

## Key Insights

1. The grasshopper stops if it jumps outside the bounds [0, n-1]
2. If it enters a cycle (visits the same cell twice), it will jump forever → INFINITE
3. We need to track visited positions to detect cycles

## Algorithm Steps

1. Start at position 0
2. Keep track of visited positions in a set
3. For each position:
   - Get the direction and jump length
   - Calculate next position
   - If next position is outside [0, n-1]: return "FINITE"
   - If next position was already visited: return "INFINITE" (cycle detected)
   - Mark current position as visited and move to next position
4. Continue until we either escape or detect a cycle

## Edge Cases to Consider

- Jump length can be very large (up to 10^9)
- Need to handle the direction correctly: '<' means left, '>' means right
- Position boundaries: 0 to n-1 (inclusive)

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
current_pos = 0

while True:
    # Check if current position is out of bounds
    if current_pos < 0 or current_pos >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # If we've been here before, we're in a cycle
    if current_pos in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(current_pos)
    
    # Get direction and jump length
    dir_char = directions[current_pos]
    jump_len = jumps[current_pos]
    
    # Calculate next position based on direction
    if dir_char == '>':
        next_pos = current_pos + jump_len
    else:  # dir_char == '<'
        next_pos = current_pos - jump_len
    
    # Update current position
    current_pos = next_pos
    
    # Check if next position is out of bounds
    if current_pos < 0 or current_pos >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of n cells, each containing a direction ("<" or ">") and a jump length
2. The grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0)
3. From each cell i, it jumps according to the direction and length:
   - ">" means jump right by di cells
   - "<" means jump left by di cells
4. The grasshopper stops if it jumps outside the strip (index < 0 or index >= n)
5. We need to determine if the journey is finite (stops) or infinite (loops)

## Key Insight

The grasshopper will be infinite if:
1. It enters a cycle (visits the same cell twice)
2. It keeps jumping within bounds without escaping

We need to simulate the jumps and track which cells we've visited. If we ever visit a cell we've seen before, we have a cycle → infinite. If we jump outside the bounds, it's finite.

## Algorithm

1. Start at position 0
2. Use a set to keep track of visited positions
3. For each step:
   - Check if current position is outside bounds → return "FINITE"
   - If we've visited this position before → return "INFINITE"
   - Mark current position as visited
   - Calculate next position based on direction and jump length
   - Move to next position
4. Continue until we either escape or find a cycle

## Edge Cases

- Jump length could be very large (up to 10^9), so we need to check bounds first
- The grasshopper might jump to a position that's outside bounds immediately

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Set to keep track of visited positions
visited = set()
current_pos = 0

while True:
    # Check if current position is out of bounds
    if current_pos < 0 or current_pos >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # If we've been to this position before, we have a cycle
    if current_pos in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(current_pos)
    
    # Calculate next position based on direction
    if directions[current_pos] == '>':
        next_pos = current_pos + jumps[current_pos]
    else:  # directions[current_pos] == '<'
        next_pos = current_pos - jumps[current_pos]
    
    # Move to next position
    current_pos = next_pos
```

## How it works with examples:

**Example 1:** n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]
- Start at pos 0: direction '>' → jump to 0+1 = 1
- At pos 1: direction '<' → jump to 1-2 = -1 (out of bounds)
- Output: "FINITE"

**Example 2:** n=3, directions=">> <", jumps=[2,1,1]
- Start at pos 0: direction '>' → jump to 0+2 = 2
- At pos 2: direction '<' → jump to 2-1 = 1
- At pos 1: direction '>' → jump to 1+1 = 2
- At pos 2: already visited → infinite loop
- Output: "INFINITE"

The algorithm correctly identifies both cases.

3. From each cell, it jumps according to the direction and length
4. If the jump takes it outside the strip [0, n-1], it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper jumps forever (INFINITE) or eventually jumps out (FINITE)

## Key Insights

1. If the grasshopper ever jumps outside the strip, the answer is "FINITE"
2. If it visits the same cell twice, it will enter an infinite loop (since the path from that cell is deterministic)
3. So we need to detect cycles in the path
4. If we visit a cell we've seen before, we have an infinite loop → "INFINITE"
5. If we jump out of bounds, we stop → "FINITE"

## Algorithm

1. Start at position 0
2. Keep track of visited positions in a set
3. At each step:
   - Check if current position is out of bounds (negative or ≥ n) → return "FINITE"
   - Check if we've visited this position before → return "INFINITE"
   - Mark current position as visited
   - Calculate next position based on direction and jump length
   - Move to next position and repeat

## Step-by-step Solution

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]
- Start at position 0
- From cell 0: direction '>' means jump right by 1 → new position = 0 + 1 = 1
- From cell 1: direction '<' means jump left by 2 → new position = 1 - 2 = -1
- Position -1 is out of bounds → "FINITE"

**Example 2**: n=3, directions=">> <", jumps=[2,1,1]
- Start at position 0
- From cell 0: direction '>' means jump right by 2 → new position = 0 + 2 = 2
- From cell 2: direction '<' means jump left by 1 → new position = 2 - 1 = 1
- From cell 1: direction '>' means jump right by 1 → new position = 1 + 1 = 2
- We've already visited position 2 → cycle detected → "INFINITE"

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
position = 0

while True:
    # If position is out of bounds, grasshopper stops
    if position < 0 or position >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # If we've visited this position before, we have a cycle
    if position in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(position)
    
    # Calculate next position based on current direction and jump length
    if directions[position] == '>':
        position += jumps[position]
    else:  # directions[position] == '<'
        position -= jumps[position]
```

The algorithm works as follows:
1. We maintain a set of visited positions to detect cycles
2. We start at position 0
3. At each step, we check if we're out of bounds (FINITE) or if we've been here before (INFINITE)
4. If neither, we move to the next position according to the direction and jump length
5. We continue until we either jump out of bounds or detect a cycle

Time complexity: O(n) in the worst case (we visit at most n distinct positions)
Space complexity: O(n) for the visited set

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of n cells, each containing a direction ("><") and a jump length
2. The grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0)
3. From each cell i, it jumps according to the direction and length
4. If the grasshopper jumps outside the strip (to index < 0 or >= n), it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper will jump forever (INFINITE) or eventually exit (FINITE)

## Key Insight

The grasshopper will jump forever only if it enters a cycle - meaning it visits the same sequence of cells repeatedly.

So the problem reduces to: **Does the grasshopper enter a cycle?**

## Algorithm

1. Simulate the grasshopper's movement step by step
2. Keep track of visited positions
3. If we ever visit a position we've seen before, we have a cycle → INFINITE
4. If we jump outside the range [0, n-1], we exit → FINITE

## Step-by-step approach:

1. Read input: n, direction string, jump lengths
2. Start at position 0
3. Use a set to track visited positions
4. For each step:
   - Check if current position is outside bounds (already handled by the simulation)
   - Check if we've visited this position before
   - If yes, we have a cycle → return "INFINITE"
   - If no, mark position as visited and jump to next position
5. If we exit the strip, return "FINITE"

## Edge cases to consider:

- n = 1: only one cell, jump from cell 0
- When jumping, check bounds after calculating new position
- Cycles can happen in any configuration

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
position = 0

while True:
    # Check if current position is out of bounds
    if position < 0 or position >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # If we've been here before, we have a cycle
    if position in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(position)
    
    # Calculate next position based on direction and jump length
    if directions[position] == '>':
        next_position = position + jumps[position]
    else:  # directions[position] == '<'
        next_position = position - jumps[position]
    
    # Move to next position
    position = next_position
```

## Verification with examples:

**Example 1:** n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]
- Start at position 0
- directions[0] = '>', so jump to 0+1 = 1
- position 1: directions[1] = '<', so jump to 1-2 = -1
- -1 < 0, so FINITE ✓

**Example 2:** n=3, directions=">> <", jumps=[2,1,1]
- Start at position 0
- directions[0] = '>', so jump to 0+2 = 2
- position 2: directions[2] = '<', so jump to 2-1 = 1
- position 1: directions[1] = '>', so jump to 1+1 = 2
- position 2: already visited! → INFINITE ✓

The solution correctly handles both cases.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of n cells, each containing a direction ('>' or '<') and a jump length
2. Grasshopper starts at cell 0 (first cell)
3. From each cell i, it jumps according to the direction and length
4. If the grasshopper jumps outside the strip (to index < 0 or >= n), it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper will eventually jump out (FINITE) or keep jumping forever (INFINITE)

## Key Insight

The grasshopper will jump forever if it enters a cycle - it keeps visiting the same cells in a repeating pattern.

If the grasshopper jumps out of bounds, the answer is "FINITE".
If the grasshopper keeps visiting cells in a cycle, the answer is "INFINITE".

## Approach

1. Simulate the grasshopper's movement step by step
2. Keep track of which cells we've visited
3. If we visit a cell we've seen before, we've entered a cycle → "INFINITE"
4. If we jump outside the bounds, we stop → "FINITE"

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Read input: n, directions string, jump lengths
2. Start at position 0
3. Use a set to track visited positions
4. While the current position is within bounds:
   - If we've visited this position before → cycle detected → INFINITE
   - Otherwise, mark it as visited
   - Calculate next position based on direction and jump length
   - Move to next position
5. If we exit bounds, return FINITE

## Edge Cases

- Single cell: if direction is '<' or '>' and jump length > 0, will jump out
- Position 0: jumping right might go out of bounds
- Position n-1: jumping left might go out of bounds

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
pos = 0

while 0 <= pos < n:
    if pos in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        exit()
    
    visited.add(pos)
    
    # Determine next position based on direction
    if directions[pos] == '>':
        pos = pos + jumps[pos]
    else:  # directions[pos] == '<'
        pos = pos - jumps[pos]

# If we exit the loop, grasshopper jumped out of bounds
print("FINITE")
```

## Trace through examples:

**Example 1:** n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]
- Start at pos=0, not visited → add to visited
- directions[0]='>' → pos = 0 + 1 = 1
- pos=1, not visited → add to visited  
- directions[1]='<' → pos = 1 - 2 = -1
- pos=-1 < 0 → exit loop → "FINITE" ✓

**Example 2:** n=3, directions=">> <", jumps=[2,1,1]
- Start at pos=0, not visited → add to visited
- directions[0]='>' → pos = 0 + 2 = 2
- pos=2, not visited → add to visited
- directions[2]='<' → pos = 2 - 1 = 1
- pos=1, not visited → add to visited
- directions[1]='>' → pos = 1 + 1 = 2
- pos=2, already visited → "INFINITE" ✓

The solution correctly handles both cases.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

There are n TV shows you want to watch. Suppose the whole time is split into equal parts called "minutes". The i-th of the shows is going from l_i-th to r_i-th minute, both ends inclusive.

You need a TV to watch a TV show and you can't watch two TV shows which air at the same time on the same TV, so it is possible you will need multiple TVs in some minutes. For example, if segments [l_i, r_i] and [l_j, r_j] intersect, then shows i and j can't be watched simultaneously on one TV.

Once you start watching a show on some TV it is not possible to "move" it to another TV (since it would be too distracting), or to watch another show on the same TV until this show ends.

There is a TV Rental shop near you. It rents a TV for x rupees, and charges y (y < x) rupees for every extra minute you keep the TV. So in order to rent a TV for minutes [a; b] you will need to pay x + y ⋅ (b - a). 

You can assume, that taking and returning of the TV doesn't take any time and doesn't distract from watching other TV shows. Find the minimum possible cost to view all shows. Since this value could be too large, print it modulo 10^9 + 7.

Input

The first line contains integers n, x and y (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5, 1 ≤ y < x ≤ 10^9) — the number of TV shows, the cost to rent a TV for the first minute and the cost to rent a TV for every subsequent minute.

Each of the next n lines contains two integers l_i and r_i (1 ≤ l_i ≤ r_i ≤ 10^9) denoting the start and the end minute of the i-th TV show.

Output

Print exactly one integer — the minimum cost to view all the shows taken modulo 10^9 + 7.

Examples

Input

5 4 3
1 2
4 10
2 4
10 11
5 9


Output

60

Input

6 3 2
8 20
6 22
4 15
20 28
17 25
20 27


Output

142

Input

2 1000000000 2
1 2
2 3


Output

999999997

Note

In the first example, the optimal strategy would be to rent 3 TVs to watch:

  * Show [1, 2] on the first TV,
  * Show [4, 10] on the second TV,
  * Shows [2, 4], [5, 9], [10, 11] on the third TV. 



This way the cost for the first TV is 4 + 3 ⋅ (2 - 1) = 7, for the second is 4 + 3 ⋅ (10 - 4) = 22 and for the third is 4 + 3 ⋅ (11 - 2) = 31, which gives 60 int total.

In the second example, it is optimal watch each show on a new TV.

In third example, it is optimal to watch both shows on a new TV. I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n TV shows with time intervals [l_i, r_i]
2. We need to assign shows to TVs such that no two shows overlap on the same TV
3. The cost to rent a TV for minutes [a, b] is x + y*(b-a)
4. We want to minimize total cost
5. Answer modulo 10^9 + 7

## Key Insights

1. This is a classic interval scheduling problem where we need to find the minimum number of TVs needed
2. The optimal strategy is to use as few TVs as possible - we want to minimize the number of TVs since each TV has a base cost x
3. We can use a greedy approach: sort the intervals by start time, and use a heap to track end times of currently active shows
4. For each show, if its start time is after the earliest ending show, we can reuse that TV; otherwise, we need a new TV

## Algorithm Steps

1. Sort all shows by start time
2. Use a min-heap to keep track of end times of shows currently active on TVs
3. For each show in sorted order:
   - If the earliest ending show in our heap has end time ≤ current show's start time, we can reuse that TV
   - Otherwise, we need to rent a new TV
4. The total cost is the sum of x (base cost) plus y*(end_time - start_time) for each TV assignment

Actually, for each TV we use, we pay:
- x for the first minute
- y for each additional minute (so total cost for [a,b] is x + y*(b-a))

## Refined Approach

1. We need to find the minimum number of TVs needed (this is the "interval chromatic number")
2. We can use a greedy interval scheduling with a heap
3. After determining the number of TVs needed, we need to calculate the total cost

Actually, I think I need to reconsider the cost calculation. The key insight is that we want to minimize the total cost, which means we want to minimize the sum of (x + y*(end-start)) for all TVs.

But the optimal assignment might not be obvious. Let me think about this differently.

The minimum number of TVs needed is determined by the maximum number of overlapping intervals at any point. We can use a sweep line algorithm:

1. Create events (l_i, +1) and (r_i+1, -1) for each show
2. Sweep through time and track the current number of overlapping shows
3. The maximum value during the sweep is the minimum number of TVs needed

Then, the total cost is: (number of TVs) * x + y * (sum of all time intervals covered by each TV)

But the second part is complex - we need to calculate the total time each TV is used.

Actually, let me implement the standard greedy algorithm:

```python
import heapq

def solve():
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    n, x, y = map(int, input().split())
    shows = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        shows.append((l, r))
    
    # Sort by start time
    shows.sort()
    
    # Use a min heap to track end times of currently active shows
    heap = []
    total_cost = 0
    
    for start, end in shows:
        # If the earliest ending show ends before or at current start time,
        # we can reuse that TV
        if heap and heap[0] <= start:
            heapq.heappop(heap)
        
        # Add current show to a TV
        heapq.heappush(heap, end)
        
        # Calculate the cost for this TV assignment
        # Actually, we need to track the cost more carefully
        
    # Wait, this approach doesn't directly give us the cost
    
    # Let me change approach - we need to calculate total cost
    # For each TV, the cost is x + y*(duration)
    
    # We need to find the minimum number of TVs, and then calculate the total duration
    # But the total duration depends on how we assign shows to TVs
    
    # Standard solution for this type of problem:
    # 1. Find minimum number of TVs needed using greedy
    # 2. The cost is the sum of base costs + additional costs
    
    # Actually, let me implement the standard solution:
    
    # Sort by start time
    shows.sort()
    
    # Min heap to store end times of shows on each TV
    end_times = []
    total_cost = 0
    
    for start, end in shows:
        # Remove TVs that have ended before current show starts
        while end_times and end_times[0] <= start:
            heapq.heappop(end_times)
        
        # Add the current show's end time to the heap
        heapq.heappush(end_times, end)
        
        # The cost contribution: we're using one more TV
        # For this show, we need to pay the cost for its duration
        # But we need to track the total duration across all TVs
        
        # Actually, the total duration across all TVs is the sum of (end - start) for all shows
        # plus the base cost for each TV
    
    # The base cost is x * (number of TVs)
    # The additional cost is y * (total duration of all assignments)
    
    # But in our assignment, we're not tracking total duration
    
    # Let me think differently - the total duration is the sum of all (r_i - l_i)
    # plus the base cost for each TV
    
    # Actually, no - each TV might cover multiple shows or just one
    
    # Standard approach: the cost for each TV is x + y * (duration)
    # So total cost = (# of TVs) * x + y * (sum of durations of all TVs)
    
    # The sum of durations of all TVs is the sum of (end - start) for all shows
    # because each show contributes its duration exactly once
    
    # But wait - if a TV watches multiple shows, the total duration is the sum of individual show durations
    
    # So total cost = (# of TVs) * x + y * (sum of (r_i - l_i) for all shows)
    
    # Therefore: total_cost = (number_of_tvs) * x + y * (total_duration)
    
    # We need to find minimum number of TVs
    
    # How to find minimum number of TVs - it's the maximum overlap at any point
    
    events = []
    for l, r in shows:
        events.append((l, 1))      # start event
        events.append((r + 1, -1)) # end event (shifted by +1 to avoid overlap issues)
    
    events.sort()
    
    max_overlap = 0
    current_overlap = 0
    
    for time, delta in events:
        current_overlap += delta
        max_overlap = max(max_overlap, current_overlap)
    
    # Total duration is sum of (r_i - l_i) for all shows
    total_duration = 0
    for l, r in shows:
        total_duration += (r - l)
    
    total_cost = max_overlap * x + y * total_duration
    
    return total_cost % MOD

print(solve())
```

Wait, I need to verify this logic. 

The key insight is that we want to minimize the number of TVs. The minimum number of TVs needed is the maximum number of overlapping intervals at any point in time. This is a well-known result in interval scheduling.

For each TV we use, we pay:
- Base cost: x (for the first minute)
- Additional cost: y * (duration of that TV's usage)

The total duration across all TVs is the sum of (r_i - l_i) for all shows, because each show contributes its duration exactly once.

Therefore, the total cost is: (number of TVs) * x + y * (sum of all show durations)

```python
import heapq

MOD = 10**9 + 7

def solve():
    n, x, y = map(int, input().split())
    shows = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        shows.append((l, r))
    
    # Create events for sweep line
    events = []
    for l, r in shows:
        events.append((l, 1))       # start of show
        events.append((r + 1, -1))  # end of show (end time is r+1 to avoid overlap)
    
    # Sort events by time
    events.sort()
    
    # Find maximum overlap (minimum number of TVs needed)
    max_overlap = 0
    current_overlap = 0
    
    for time, delta in events:
        current_overlap += delta
        max_overlap = max(max_overlap, current_overlap)
    
    # Calculate total duration of all shows
    total_duration = 0
    for l, r in shows:
        total_duration += (r - l)
    
    # Total cost = (number of TVs) * base cost + (duration) * per-minute cost
    total_cost = max_overlap * x + y * total_duration
    
    return total_cost % MOD

print(solve())
```

import heapq

MOD = 10**9 + 7

def solve():
    n, x, y = map(int, input().split())
    shows = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        shows.append((l, r))
    
    # Create events for sweep line
    events = []
    for l, r in shows:
        events.append((l, 1))       # start of show
        events.append((r + 1, -1))  # end of show (end time is r+1 to avoid overlap)
    
    # Sort events by time
    events.sort()
    
    # Find maximum overlap (minimum number of TVs needed)
    max_overlap = 0
    current_overlap = 0
    
    for time, delta in events:
        current_overlap += delta
        max_overlap = max(max_overlap, current_overlap)
    
    # Calculate total duration of all shows
    total_duration = 0
    for l, r in shows:
        total_duration += (r - l)
    
    # Total cost = (number of TVs) * base cost + (duration) * per-minute cost
    total_cost = max_overlap * x + y * total_duration
    
    return total_cost % MOD

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

There are n TV shows you want to watch. Suppose the whole time is split into equal parts called "minutes". The i-th of the shows is going from l_i-th to r_i-th minute, both ends inclusive.

You need a TV to watch a TV show and you can't watch two TV shows which air at the same time on the same TV, so it is possible you will need multiple TVs in some minutes. For example, if segments [l_i, r_i] and [l_j, r_j] intersect, then shows i and j can't be watched simultaneously on one TV.

Once you start watching a show on some TV it is not possible to "move" it to another TV (since it would be too distracting), or to watch another show on the same TV until this show ends.

There is a TV Rental shop near you. It rents a TV for x rupees, and charges y (y < x) rupees for every extra minute you keep the TV. So in order to rent a TV for minutes [a; b] you will need to pay x + y ⋅ (b - a). 

You can assume, that taking and returning of the TV doesn't take any time and doesn't distract from watching other TV shows. Find the minimum possible cost to view all shows. Since this value could be too large, print it modulo 10^9 + 7.

Input

The first line contains integers n, x and y (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5, 1 ≤ y < x ≤ 10^9) — the number of TV shows, the cost to rent a TV for the first minute and the cost to rent a TV for every subsequent minute.

Each of the next n lines contains two integers l_i and r_i (1 ≤ l_i ≤ r_i ≤ 10^9) denoting the start and the end minute of the i-th TV show.

Output

Print exactly one integer — the minimum cost to view all the shows taken modulo 10^9 + 7.

Examples

Input

5 4 3
1 2
4 10
2 4
10 11
5 9


Output

60

Input

6 3 2
8 20
6 22
4 15
20 28
17 25
20 27


Output

142

Input

2 1000000000 2
1 2
2 3


Output

999999997

Note

In the first example, the optimal strategy would be to rent 3 TVs to watch:

  * Show [1, 2] on the first TV,
  * Show [4, 10] on the second TV,
  * Shows [2, 4], [5, 9], [10, 11] on the third TV. 



This way the cost for the first TV is 4 + 3 ⋅ (2 - 1) = 7, for the second is 4 + 3 ⋅ (10 - 4) = 22 and for the third is 4 + 3 ⋅ (11 - 2) = 31, which gives 60 int total.

In the second example, it is optimal watch each show on a new TV.

In third example, it is optimal to watch both shows on a new TV.
```python
import sys
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n, x, y = map(int, input().split())
    shows = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        shows.append((l, r))
    
    # Sort by start time
    shows.sort()
    
    # We'll use a greedy algorithm to find the minimum cost
    # The idea is that we want to minimize the total cost
    
    # Sort by end time to use interval scheduling
    shows.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])
    
    # Use a greedy approach to merge overlapping intervals
    merged = []
    
    for l, r in shows:
        if not merged or merged[-1][1] < l:
            # No overlap, add as new interval
            merged.append((l, r))
        else:
            # Overlap, merge with the last interval
            merged[-1] = (merged[-1][0], max(merged[-1][1], r))
    
    # Calculate total cost
    total_cost = 0
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    for l, r in merged:
        cost = x + y * (r - l)
        total_cost = (total_cost + cost) % MOD
    
    print(total_cost)

solve()
```

```python
    shows.append((l, r))
```

```python
shows.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

# We'll maintain a list of TVs, each represented by (start, end)
tv_intervals = []

    # Look for a TV that ends before l
    found = False
    for i in range(len(tv_intervals)):
        if tv_intervals[i][1] < l:

            found = True
```

```python
n, x, y = map(int, input().split())
shows = []
for _ in range(n):
    l, r = map(int, input().split())
    shows.append((l, r))

# Sort by end time
shows.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])

# We'll maintain a list of TVs, each represented by (min_start, max_end)
tv_intervals = []

for l, r in shows:
    # Look for a TV that ends before l (i.e., can be reused)
    found = False
    for i in range(len(tv_intervals)):
        min_start, max_end = tv_intervals[i]
        if max_end < l:
            # This TV is free, we can reuse it
            # Update the end time to r, and also update the start time to min(min_start, l)
            # But we need to know the new min_start and max_end
            new_min_start = min(min_start, l)
            new_max_end = max(max_end, r)
            tv_intervals[i] = (new_min_start, new_max_end)
            found = True
            break
    
    if not found:
        # Create new TV
        tv_intervals.append((l, r))

# Calculate total cost
total_cost = 0
MOD = 10**9 + 7

for min_start, max_end in tv_intervals:
    cost = x + y * (max_end - min_start)
    total_cost = (total_cost + cost) % MOD

print(total_cost)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

There are n TV shows you want to watch. Suppose the whole time is split into equal parts called "minutes". The i-th of the shows is going from l_i-th to r_i-th minute, both ends inclusive.

You need a TV to watch a TV show and you can't watch two TV shows which air at the same time on the same TV, so it is possible you will need multiple TVs in some minutes. For example, if segments [l_i, r_i] and [l_j, r_j] intersect, then shows i and j can't be watched simultaneously on one TV.

Once you start watching a show on some TV it is not possible to "move" it to another TV (since it would be too distracting), or to watch another show on the same TV until this show ends.

There is a TV Rental shop near you. It rents a TV for x rupees, and charges y (y < x) rupees for every extra minute you keep the TV. So in order to rent a TV for minutes [a; b] you will need to pay x + y ⋅ (b - a). 

You can assume, that taking and returning of the TV doesn't take any time and doesn't distract from watching other TV shows. Find the minimum possible cost to view all shows. Since this value could be too large, print it modulo 10^9 + 7.

Input

The first line contains integers n, x and y (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5, 1 ≤ y < x ≤ 10^9) — the number of TV shows, the cost to rent a TV for the first minute and the cost to rent a TV for every subsequent minute.

Each of the next n lines contains two integers l_i and r_i (1 ≤ l_i ≤ r_i ≤ 10^9) denoting the start and the end minute of the i-th TV show.

Output

Print exactly one integer — the minimum cost to view all the shows taken modulo 10^9 + 7.

Examples

Input

5 4 3
1 2
4 10
2 4
10 11
5 9


Output

60

Input

6 3 2
8 20
6 22
4 15
20 28
17 25
20 27


Output

142

Input

2 1000000000 2
1 2
2 3


Output

999999997

Note

In the first example, the optimal strategy would be to rent 3 TVs to watch:

  * Show [1, 2] on the first TV,
  * Show [4, 10] on the second TV,
  * Shows [2, 4], [5, 9], [10, 11] on the third TV. 



This way the cost for the first TV is 4 + 3 ⋅ (2 - 1) = 7, for the second is 4 + 3 ⋅ (10 - 4) = 22 and for the third is 4 + 3 ⋅ (11 - 2) = 31, which gives 60 int total.

In the second example, it is optimal watch each show on a new TV.

In third example, it is optimal to watch both shows on a new TV. I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n TV shows, each airing from minute `l_i` to `r_i` (inclusive)
2. We need TVs to watch shows - cannot watch two shows simultaneously on the same TV
3. TV rental cost: `x` rupees for first minute + `y` rupees for each additional minute
4. Cost for a TV used from minute `a` to `b` is: `x + y * (b - a)`
5. We want to minimize total cost

## Key Insights

1. This is essentially a "minimum cost interval covering" problem
2. We need to find the optimal way to assign shows to TVs such that no two shows on the same TV overlap
3. For each TV, if it watches shows from time `a` to `b`, the cost is `x + y * (b - a)`
4. The key insight is that we want to minimize the total cost, which means we want to minimize the sum of `(b - a)` for all TVs, since `x` is constant per TV.

## Strategy

This is a greedy interval scheduling problem. The optimal approach is:

1. Sort shows by their end times
2. Use a greedy approach to assign shows to TVs
3. For each show, assign it to an existing TV if it doesn't overlap with any show currently assigned to that TV, otherwise create a new TV

But actually, since we want to minimize the total cost and the cost depends on the duration of each TV, we need to think differently.

I realize that the cost for a TV is `x + y * (duration)`. So we want to minimize the total duration of all TVs.

The key insight is that we should use a greedy algorithm that processes shows in order of their end times, and for each show, we assign it to a TV that ends as late as possible but doesn't conflict with the current show.

This is a classic "minimum number of intervals to cover" problem with a twist.

1. Sort shows by their end times
2. For each show in order, if it doesn't conflict with the last show assigned to the same TV, we can extend that TV
3. Otherwise, we need a new TV

- [1,2]: start new TV (cost: 4 + 3*1 = 7)
- [2,4]: conflicts with [1,2] (overlap at minute 2), so start new TV (cost: 4 + 3*2 = 10)
- [4,10]: overlaps with [2,4] (at minute 4), so start new TV (cost: 4 + 3*6 = 22)
- [5,9]: overlaps with [4,10] (overlap from 5-9), so we can assign to TV that ends at 10, but might be better to use existing TV
- [10,11]: starts at 10, so no overlap with [4,10] (end at 10), so can share TV with [4,10] (cost for [4,11]: 4 + 3*1 = 7)

- [2,4] to [5,9]: no overlap (4 to 5 is gap)
- [5,9] to [10,11]: gap at 9-10

So yes, we can chain non-overlapping shows on the same TV.

## Algorithm

1. Sort shows by end time
2. Use a greedy algorithm: maintain the end time of each active TV
3. For each show, try to assign it to a TV whose end time is <= current show's start time
4. If no such TV, create a new TV
5. The TV that ends earliest is optimal (minimizes total duration)

Actually, I think the optimal greedy strategy is: process shows in order of end time, and for each show, assign it to a TV that ends at or before the current show's start time. To minimize cost, we should assign it to a TV that ends earliest (to save time for future shows).

But since we want to minimize total duration, and all TVs start at cost `x`, we should minimize the sum of `(end_time - start_time)` for all TVs.

The key insight: we can use a greedy algorithm that processes shows in order of end time and maintains the end times of TVs. For each show, we find the TV that ends earliest but is still less than or equal to the current show's start time, and assign it to that TV. If no such TV, create a new TV.

This is the interval scheduling problem with minimum number of intervals.

```python
import sys

def solve():
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    n, x, y = map(int, input().split())
    shows = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        shows.append((l, r))
    
    # Sort shows by end time
    shows.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])
    
    # We'll track the end times of TVs we've created
    tv_end_times = []
    
    total_cost = 0
    
    for start, end in shows:
        # Find a TV that ends at or before start (so we can assign this show to it)
        # We want to find the TV that ends earliest but <= start
        pos = -1
        for i, tv_end in enumerate(tv_end_times):
            if tv_end <= start:
                pos = i
                break
        
        if pos == -1:
            # No TV available, create a new one
            total_cost = (total_cost + x + y * (end - start)) % MOD
            tv_end_times.append(end)
        else:
            # Assign to existing TV, update its end time
            tv_end_times[pos] = end
            total_cost = (total_cost + y * (end - tv_end_times[pos])) % MOD
    
    print(total_cost)

solve()
```

Wait, I made a mistake. If we assign a show to an existing TV, we're not adding to the cost for the TV's duration - we're just updating its end time. But the cost should be calculated as the total duration of the TV, not per show.

Actually, let me reconsider. The TV rental cost is for the entire duration from first minute to last minute. So if a TV watches multiple shows, the cost is `x + y * (last_end_time - first_start_time)`.

So I need to track for each TV its start and end times.

```python
import sys

def solve():
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    n, x, y = map(int, input().split())
    shows = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        shows.append((l, r))
    
    # Sort shows by end time
    shows.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])
    
    # We'll track the start and end times of TVs we've created
    # Each TV has a start_time and end_time
    tvs = []  # list of (start, end)
    
    total_cost = 0
    
    for start, end in shows:
        # Find a TV that ends at or before start (so we can assign this show to it)
        found = False
        for i in range(len(tvs)):
            tv_start, tv_end = tvs[i]
            if tv_end <= start:
                # We can assign this show to TV i
                # Update the TV's end time to end
                tvs[i] = (tv_start, end)
                # The cost for this TV is x + y * (end - tv_start)
                # We need to accumulate the total cost
                # But we're already including the original cost
                # So we need to recalculate the actual cost
                found = True
                break
        
        if not found:
            # Create a new TV
            tvs.append((start, end))
            total_cost = (total_cost + x + y * (end - start)) % MOD
        else:
            # Assign to existing TV
            # The cost change is: old cost + y * (new_end - old_end) - y * (old_end - old_start)
            # Actually, we should just maintain the total cost
            # But we don't have the original cost stored
            
            # Let me recalculate differently - just maintain the TV segments
            # and when adding a show, we just update the end time
            # The cost for each TV is x + y * (end - start)
            pass
    
    # This approach is getting messy. Let me restart.
    
    # Better approach: maintain the end times of TVs, and keep track of start time
    # We'll store end times for each TV
    
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    n, x, y = map(int, input().split())
    shows = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        shows.append((l, r))
    
    shows.sort(key=lambda x: x[1])
    
    # For each TV, we track its end time
    # When we assign a show to a TV, we update the TV's end time to the current show's end
    tv_end_times = []
    total_cost = 0
    
    for start, end in shows:
        # Find the TV with earliest end time that doesn't conflict
        # (i.e., TV end <= start)
        target_idx = -1
        min_end = float('inf')
        
        for i, tv_end in enumerate(tv_end_times):
            if tv_end <= start:
                if tv_end < min_end:
                    min_end = tv_end
                    target_idx = i
        
        if target_idx == -1:
            # No TV available, create new TV
            total_cost = (total_cost + x + y * (end - start)) % MOD
            tv_end_times.append(end)
        else:
            # Assign to existing TV
            # The TV will now end at 'end'
            # The cost change is: y * (end - tv_end) 
            # But we need to remove the old cost and add new cost
            # However, since we're not storing per-TV cost, we need to track properly
            
            # Actually, we should just calculate the final cost at the end
            # Let me store the actual TV segments instead
            
            # Just update the end time
            tv_end_times[target_idx] = end
    
    # Now calculate the total cost based on TV segments
    total_cost = 0
    tv_segments = []

    for start, end in shows:

        target_idx = -1
        min_end = float('inf')
        
        for i, tv_end in enumerate(tv_end_times):
            if tv_end <= start:
                if tv_end < min_end:
                    min_end = tv_end
                    target_idx = i

            # Create new TV
            tv_end_times.append(end)
        else:
            # Update existing TV
            tv_end_times[target_idx] = end
    
    # Now calculate final cost
    total_cost = 0
    for tv_end in tv_end_times:
        # Need to know start time for each TV
        # This won't work with just end times

        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        shows.append((l, r))

    # Initially no TVs
    tvs = []  # each element is (start, end)

    for start, end in shows:
        # Find a TV that ends at or before start
        # We want to assign to TV that ends earliest (greedy)
        target_idx = -1
        min_end = float('inf')
        
        for i, (tv_start, tv_end) in enumerate(tvs):
            if tv_end <= start:
                if tv_end < min_end:
                    min_end = tv_end
                    target_idx = i
        
        if target_idx == -1:
            # Create new TV
            total_cost = (total_cost + x + y * (end - start)) % MOD
            tvs.append((start, end))
        else:
            # Assign to existing TV: update its end time
            tvs[target_idx] = (tvs[target_idx][0], end)
            # The cost change: we're extending the TV from tv_end to end
            # Cost increase is y * (end - tv_end)
            cost_increase = y * (end - tvs[target_idx][1])
            tvs[target_idx] = (tvs[target_idx][0], end)
            total_cost = (total_cost + cost_increase) % MOD
    
    print(total_cost % MOD)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

There are n TV shows you want to watch. Suppose the whole time is split into equal parts called "minutes". The i-th of the shows is going from l_i-th to r_i-th minute, both ends inclusive.

You need a TV to watch a TV show and you can't watch two TV shows which air at the same time on the same TV, so it is possible you will need multiple TVs in some minutes. For example, if segments [l_i, r_i] and [l_j, r_j] intersect, then shows i and j can't be watched simultaneously on one TV.

Once you start watching a show on some TV it is not possible to "move" it to another TV (since it would be too distracting), or to watch another show on the same TV until this show ends.

There is a TV Rental shop near you. It rents a TV for x rupees, and charges y (y < x) rupees for every extra minute you keep the TV. So in order to rent a TV for minutes [a; b] you will need to pay x + y ⋅ (b - a). 

You can assume, that taking and returning of the TV doesn't take any time and doesn't distract from watching other TV shows. Find the minimum possible cost to view all shows. Since this value could be too large, print it modulo 10^9 + 7.

Input

The first line contains integers n, x and y (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5, 1 ≤ y < x ≤ 10^9) — the number of TV shows, the cost to rent a TV for the first minute and the cost to rent a TV for every subsequent minute.

Each of the next n lines contains two integers l_i and r_i (1 ≤ l_i ≤ r_i ≤ 10^9) denoting the start and the end minute of the i-th TV show.

Output

Print exactly one integer — the minimum cost to view all the shows taken modulo 10^9 + 7.

Examples

Input

5 4 3
1 2
4 10
2 4
10 11
5 9


Output

60

Input

6 3 2
8 20
6 22
4 15
20 28
17 25
20 27


Output

142

Input

2 1000000000 2
1 2
2 3


Output

999999997

Note

In the first example, the optimal strategy would be to rent 3 TVs to watch:

  * Show [1, 2] on the first TV,
  * Show [4, 10] on the second TV,
  * Shows [2, 4], [5, 9], [10, 11] on the third TV. 



This way the cost for the first TV is 4 + 3 ⋅ (2 - 1) = 7, for the second is 4 + 3 ⋅ (10 - 4) = 22 and for the third is 4 + 3 ⋅ (11 - 2) = 31, which gives 60 int total.

In the second example, it is optimal watch each show on a new TV.

In third example, it is optimal to watch both shows on a new TV. Note that the answer is to be printed modulo 10^9 + 7.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n TV shows, each airing from minute `l_i` to `r_i` (inclusive)
2. We need to watch all shows, and can't watch two shows simultaneously on the same TV
3. TV rental cost: x rupees for the first minute + y rupees for each extra minute
4. Cost for renting a TV from minute `a` to `b` is `x + y * (b - a)`
5. We want to minimize the total cost

## Key Insights

1. This is essentially a "minimum interval covering" problem where we want to minimize the cost of TV rentals.
2. When two shows overlap, they must be watched on different TVs.
3. The problem can be viewed as finding the minimum number of TVs needed, but with a cost function that depends on the duration of each TV's rental.

## Strategy

This is a classic interval scheduling problem. The key insight is that we need to find the minimum number of TVs needed, and then assign shows to TVs optimally.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    n, x, y = map(int, input().split())
    shows = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())

    shows.sort()
    
    # Use greedy approach to find minimum TVs needed
    # We'll use a min-heap to keep track of end times of currently active TVs

        heapq.heappush(end_times, end)
        
        # Calculate cost for this TV segment
        # But we need to track when TVs start and end to calculate actual cost

    # For each time point, we need to know how many TVs are active
    
    # Alternative approach: use sweep line to calculate cost incrementally
    
    events = []
    for l, r in shows:
        events.append((l, 1))  # start of show
        events.append((r + 1, -1))  # end of show (exclusive)
    
    events.sort()
    
    total_cost = 0
    current_ovl = 0
    
    # For each time interval between events, we have a constant overlap count
    for i in range(len(events) - 1):
        time1, time2 = events[i][0], events[i + 1][0]
        if time1 >= time2:
            continue
            
        # Number of overlapping shows at any time in [time1, time2)
        overlap = current_ovl
        
        # Cost for each minute in this interval = overlap * (cost per minute)
        # Each TV contributes y rupees per minute for each extra minute
        # For a TV that runs from a to b, the cost is x + y*(b-a)
        
        # Actually, I need a different approach
        
    # Final approach: use greedy interval assignment with cost calculation

    shows.sort()
    
    # Use a greedy algorithm to assign shows to TVs
    # Minimize the number of TVs first, then the cost of each TV
    # But efficiency matters
    
    # After reading the examples, I see that when shows overlap, they need separate TVs
    # The cost for a TV is x + y * (end_time - start_time)
    
    # We want to minimize the sum of (x + y * (end - start)) over all TVs
    
    # This becomes a problem of grouping non-overlapping intervals efficiently
    
    # Actually, the optimal solution is to use a sweep line and calculate cost at each time interval
    
    # Use coordinate compression and sweep line
    
    events = []
    for l, r in shows:
        events.append((l, 1))
        events.append((r + 1, -1))
    
    events.sort()
    
    total_cost = 0
    current_count = 0
    
    # Process events chronologically
    for i in range(len(events)):
        time = events[i][0]
        change = events[i][1]
        
        # Advance to next event time
        if i < len(events) - 1:
            next_time = events[i + 1][0]
            if next_time > time:
                duration = next_time - time
                # During this interval, there are 'current_count' TVs active
                # Each TV costs y per minute for each minute it's active
                # Total cost contribution = current_count * duration * y
                total_cost = (total_cost + current_count * duration * y) % MOD
        else:
            # Last event, just update count
            pass
            
        # Update current count
        current_count += change
    
    # Add the base cost x for each TV
    # How many TVs do we have? This is the maximum number of overlapping shows
    max_overlap = 0
    current_count = 0
    for time, change in events:
        if current_count > max_overlap:
            max_overlap = current_count
        current_count += change
    
    total_cost = (total_cost + max_overlap * x) % MOD
    
    return total_cost
```

Wait, I think there's a fundamental flaw in my cost calculation. Let me reconsider.

The cost for a TV that covers from time `a` to time `b` is `x + y * (b - a)`. 

When we have overlapping shows, each show needs to be on a separate TV, and each TV contributes to the cost for the duration it's active.

The total cost should be: sum over all TVs of (x + y * (end_time - start_time))

This means:
- We get x for each TV (even if it's only active for one minute)
- We get y * (duration) for the duration each TV is active

So I need to:
1. Find the minimum number of TVs needed (which is the maximum overlap)
2. Calculate the total duration of all TV uses

But actually, the duration of a TV is the time it's actively showing a show. 

For each show, if it's assigned to a TV, the TV runs from that show's start to end. 

The optimal assignment is to assign shows to TVs such that we minimize the sum of TV rental costs.

The key insight is that we want to minimize the sum of durations of the TVs.

I think I need to use a different approach:

```python
    # Process the event
    if event_type == 1:
        current_count += 1
    else:
        current_count -= 1
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n TV shows, each showing from minute `l_i` to `r_i` (inclusive)
2. We need to assign shows to TVs such that no two shows overlap on the same TV
3. The cost to rent a TV for time interval `[a, b]` is `x + y * (b - a)`
4. We want to minimize the total cost
5. Answer should be modulo 10^9 + 7

## Key Insights

1. The cost for a TV is `x + y * (duration)` where duration is `(end - start)`
2. We want to minimize the total cost, which means we want to minimize the sum of `y * (duration)` for all TVs
3. Since `x` is charged for each TV, we want to minimize the number of TVs, but also minimize the total duration
4. The optimal strategy is to merge overlapping shows when possible

## Strategy

This is a classic interval scheduling problem. The key insight is:
- We should group overlapping intervals together and assign them to the same TV
- To minimize cost, we should greedily merge intervals that overlap
- The optimal solution is to sort intervals by start time and then merge overlapping intervals

## Algorithm Steps

1. Sort all shows by their start time
2. Use a greedy approach to merge overlapping intervals
3. For each merged group, calculate the cost: `x + y * (last_end - first_start)`
4. Sum up all costs

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    n, x, y = map(int, input().split())
    shows = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        shows.append((l, r))
    
    # Sort shows by start time
    shows.sort()
    
    # Group overlapping shows
    total_cost = 0
    i = 0
    
    while i < n:
        # Current group starts at shows[i]
        current_start = shows[i][0]
        current_end = shows[i][1]
        
        # Extend the current group to include all overlapping shows
        j = i + 1
        while j < n and shows[j][0] <= current_end:
            current_end = max(current_end, shows[j][1])
            j += 1
        
        # Calculate cost for this TV
        duration = current_end - current_start
        cost = x + y * duration
        total_cost = (total_cost + cost) % MOD
        
        i = j
    
    print(total_cost)

solve()
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

There are n TV shows you want to watch. Suppose the whole time is split into equal parts called "minutes". The i-th of the shows is going from l_i-th to r_i-th minute, both ends inclusive.

You need a TV to watch a TV show and you can't watch two TV shows which air at the same time on the same TV, so it is possible you will need multiple TVs in some minutes. For example, if segments [l_i, r_i] and [l_j, r_j] intersect, then shows i and j can't be watched simultaneously on one TV.

Once you start watching a show on some TV it is not possible to "move" it to another TV (since it would be too distracting), or to watch another show on the same TV until this show ends.

There is a TV Rental shop near you. It rents a TV for x rupees, and charges y (y < x) rupees for every extra minute you keep the TV. So in order to rent a TV for minutes [a; b] you will need to pay x + y ⋅ (b - a). 

You can assume, that taking and returning of the TV doesn't take any time and doesn't distract from watching other TV shows. Find the minimum possible cost to view all shows. Since this value could be too large, print it modulo 10^9 + 7.

Input

The first line contains integers n, x and y (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5, 1 ≤ y < x ≤ 10^9) — the number of TV shows, the cost to rent a TV for the first minute and the cost to rent a TV for every subsequent minute.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n TV shows, each airing from minute `l_i` to `r_i` (inclusive)
2. We need to rent TVs to watch these shows
3. Each TV has a base cost `x` for the first minute, and `y` per additional minute
4. If we rent a TV for time `[a, b]`, the cost is `x + y * (b - a)`
5. We cannot watch two shows simultaneously on the same TV if they overlap
6. We want to minimize the total cost

## Key Insights

1. The cost for a TV rented from minute `a` to `b` is `x + y * (b - a)`
2. This means we pay `x` regardless of the duration, plus `y` for each minute we keep the TV beyond the first minute
3. To minimize cost, we want to minimize the total "extra minutes" across all TVs
4. The problem reduces to: how to group overlapping shows so that the sum of `(end - start)` across all TVs is minimized?

## Strategy

This is essentially a interval scheduling problem. We need to find the optimal way to assign shows to TVs such that no two shows on the same TV overlap.

The key insight is that we should use a greedy approach with coordinate compression and interval merging:

1. Sort shows by their start time
2. For each time point, track which TVs are currently in use
3. When we encounter a new show, if it doesn't overlap with any currently active show, we can assign it to an existing TV
4. Otherwise, we need a new TV

However, there's a more efficient approach: we can think of this as finding the minimum number of non-overlapping TV assignments.

Actually, let me think more systematically:

The cost for a TV assigned to shows that span from time `a` to `b` is `x + y * (b - a)`.

Since we have to watch all shows, and we can't watch overlapping shows on the same TV, we need to partition the shows into non-overlapping groups.

For each group of shows that are assigned to the same TV, if they span from time `a` to time `b`, the cost is `x + y * (b - a)`.

So the total cost is: `#of_TVs * x + y * (total_extra_minutes)`

The total extra minutes = sum over all TVs of `(end_time - start_time)`.

To minimize total cost, we want to:
- Minimize the number of TVs (since each has base cost x)
- Minimize the total extra minutes (since each minute beyond the first costs y)

But these two goals conflict. We need to find the optimal balance.

## Optimal Strategy

The optimal strategy is to use a greedy interval merging approach:

1. Sort shows by their start time
2. Use a greedy algorithm to merge overlapping intervals
3. For each merged group, calculate the cost

Wait, this is not quite right. We need to assign shows to TVs optimally. For a TV to watch multiple shows, those shows must not overlap.

The minimum cost will be achieved when we minimize the sum of `(end_time - start_time)` across all TVs.

Actually, let me reconsider: the problem is asking for the minimum cost to watch all shows. The key insight is that we can use dynamic programming or greedy with interval merging.

After more thought, the optimal approach is:

1. Sort shows by start time
2. Use a greedy algorithm that maintains the current active TVs
3. For each new show, if it doesn't overlap with any active TV, assign it to one of the active TVs
4. Otherwise, start a new TV

But we need to be more precise about the cost calculation.

```python
def solve():
    n, x, y = map(int, input().split())
    shows = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        shows.append((l, r))
    
    # Sort shows by start time
    shows.sort()
    
    # We'll use a greedy approach with a priority queue or simply track active intervals
    # For each show, check if it can be assigned to an existing TV
    # We'll track the end time of each TV we have
    
    import heapq
    
    # Use a min heap to store the end times of active TVs
    # For each show, if it can fit in any existing TV, we assign it
    # Otherwise, we need a new TV
    
    active_end_times = []  # min heap of end times of active TVs
    total_cost = 0
    
    for l, r in shows:
        # Check if the current show can be assigned to any existing TV
        # If the current show overlaps with any active TV, we need a new TV
        # But we need to decide which TV to assign to

        # if the show doesn't overlap with it, otherwise create new TV
        
        # Actually, we can be smarter: we can simulate the optimal assignment
        
        # We want to minimize extra minutes, so we want to minimize the span of each TV
        # If the current show overlaps with the earliest ending TV, we need a new TV
        
        # Check if any active TV ends before or at the current show starts
        can_assign = False
        earliest_end = None
        
        # Find the TV that ends earliest and doesn't overlap with current show
        for end in active_end_times:
            if end <= l:  # No overlap - we can assign to this TV
                can_assign = True
                earliest_end = end
                break
        
        if can_assign:
            # Assign to the TV that ends earliest
            # Update that TV's end time to r
            # Find and remove the TV that ends at earliest_end
            # Then add r as the new end time
            # But we need to maintain the heap
            
            # We need to find the element with min end time
            # Remove it from heap, add r to heap
            idx = None
            for i, end_time in enumerate(active_end_times):
                if end_time == earliest_end:
                    idx = i
                    break
            
            # This approach is inefficient
            
            # Better: just maintain a heap and update

            # Actually, let's reframe: we don't need to know exact which TV, just

            # In the optimal assignment, each TV will cover a continuous interval

    # We want to minimize the sum of (end_time - start_time) over all TVs

    # Alternative insight: the minimum cost is achieved when we merge overlapping shows
    # and assign them to the same TV when possible.
    
    # So we need to find the minimum number of TVs needed, and then assign shows optimally
    
    # Actually, let's read the examples:

    # Overlaps: [1,2] overlaps with [2,4]? At minute 2 - if inclusive, they overlap at minute 2
    # So [1,2] and [2,4] overlap at minute 2
    # [2,4] and [4,10] overlap at minute 4
    # [4,10] and [5,9] overlap
    # So [1,2], [2,4], [4,10], [5,9] all overlap with each other
    # We can't put them on one TV
    
    # The optimal solution uses 3 TVs as stated.
    
    # The cost calculation: for each TV, cost = x + y * (end_time - start_time)
    
    # So the total cost is sum over TVs of (x + y * (end_time - start_time))
    # = n * x + y * (sum of extra minutes across all TVs)
    
    # We need to minimize the sum of (end_time - start_time) for each TV.
    
    # This is equivalent to: minimize the sum of lengths of the TV intervals, minus n (since each TV has a base x cost)
    
    # But we also have to ensure that no two shows on the same TV overlap.
    
    # This is a well-known problem - interval partitioning with cost minimization.
    
    # The optimal way is to use greedy: sort by start time, and assign each show to the TV that ends earliest and doesn't overlap.
    
    # We can simulate this with a min-heap of end times of active TVs.
    
    from heapq import heappush, heappop
    
    # Sort shows by start time
    shows.sort()
    
    # Min heap of end times of TVs we have
    tv_end_times = []
    total_extra_minutes = 0
    
    for l, r in shows:

            earliest_end = heappop(tv_end_times)
            heappush(tv_end_times, r)

            total_extra_minutes += (r - earliest_end)
        else:

    # The cost for a TV that watches shows from time a to time b (where a is the first time the TV is used, b is the last time) is x + y * (b - a).
    
    # In the greedy algorithm, when we assign a new show to a TV, the TV's end time is updated to max(current_end, new_end).
    # The start time of the TV remains the same.

    # Alternative approach: for each TV, the cost is x + y * (end_time - start_time)
    # We can merge TVs when possible.
    
    # From known solutions to similar problems, the greedy algorithm with min-heap of end times works.
    
    # The total cost is:
    # n * x + y * (sum of lengths of intervals covered by TVs - n)
    # because each TV has a base cost x, and the extra cost is y times the length of the interval.
    
    # But the length of the interval for a TV is (end_time - start_time).
    
    # When we assign a show to a TV, if it extends the TV from end to r, the extra length is (r - end).
    
    # So total extra minutes = sum over all TVs of (end_time - start_time) = sum of extensions when TVs are extended.
    
    # Let's implement the standard greedy interval partitioning:
    
    # Sort shows by start time
    shows.sort()
    
    # Min heap of end times of TVs
    import heapq
    tv_ends = []
    
    total_cost = 0
    
    for l, r in shows:
        # If there's a TV that ends at or before l, we can reuse it
        if tv_ends and tv_ends[0] <= l:
            # Reuse the TV that ends earliest
            earliest_end = heapq.heappop(tv_ends)
            # Extend the TV to end at r
            heapq.heappush(tv_ends, r)
            # The extra minutes for this TV is (r - earliest_end)
            # Add to total extra minutes
            total_extra_minutes = (r - earliest_end)
            # This will be multiplied by y
        else:

            heapq.heappush(tv_ends, r)
    
    # Total cost = n * x + y * total_extra_minutes
    # But is this correct?
    
    # In the example: shows [1,2], [4,10], [2,4], [10,11], [5,9]
    # Sorted: [1,2], [2,4], [4,10], [5,9], [10,11]
    
    # l=1, r=2: no TV, new TV -> tv_ends=[2]
    # l=2, r=4: tv_ends[0]=2 <= 2, reuse TV, extend to 4 -> tv_ends=[4]
    #            extra = 4-2 = 2
    # l=4, r=10: tv_ends[0]=4 <= 4, reuse TV, extend to 10 -> tv_ends=[10]
    #            extra = 10-4 = 6
    # l=5, r=9: tv_ends[0]=10 > 5, need new TV -> tv_ends=[10,9]
    #            extra += 0 (since no extension)
    # l=10, r=11: tv_ends[0]=9 <= 10, reuse TV ending at 9, extend to 11 -> tv_ends=[10,11]
    #            extra += 11-9 = 2
    # total_extra_minutes = 2+6+2 = 10
    # total_cost = 5*4 + 3*10 = 20 + 30 = 50
    
    # But expected is 60.
    
    # I see the issue: when we reuse a TV, we extend it, but the cost should include the base cost x for the TV.
    
    # The total cost should be: for each TV, x + y * (end_time - start_time)
    
    # In my calculation, I only have the extra minutes from extensions.
    
    # But the start time of the TV is not being tracked.
    
    # For a TV that is used from time a to time b, cost = x + y * (b - a)
    
    # In the greedy algorithm, when we first use a TV for a show from time l to r, the start time is l, end time is r.
    # When we extend it, the start time remains the same.
    
    # So the total extra minutes across all TVs is simply the sum of (end_time - start_time) for each TV.
    
    # But in the algorithm above, I'm only adding the extra when extending.
    
    # Actually, the total extra minutes should be the sum of all extensions.
    
    # For the first TV: from 1 to 4, extra = 3
    # For the second TV: from 4 to 10, extra = 6
    # For the third TV: from 5 to 9, extra = 4
    # For the fourth TV: from 9 to 11, extra = 2
    # Total extra = 3+6+4+2 = 15
    
    # This doesn't match.
    
    # Let me trace the events:
    # TV1: start=1, end=2 (cost = 4 + 3*(2-1) = 7)
    #       Then we extend to cover [2,4]: start=1, end=4 (cost = 4 + 3*(4-1) = 4+9=13)
    #       Then extend to cover [4,10]: start=1, end=10 (cost = 4 + 3*9 = 31)
    #       Then when we see [5,9]: need new TV from 5 to 9 (cost = 4 + 3*4 = 16)

    from heapq import heappush, heappop
    
    shows.sort()

    total_extra_length = 0
    
    for l, r in shows:
        if tv_ends and tv_ends[0] <= l:
            # Reuse a TV
            old_end = heappop(tv_ends)
            heappush(tv_ends, r)
            total_extra_length += (r - old_end)

            # New TV
            heappush(tv_ends, r)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

There are n TV shows you want to watch. Suppose the whole time is split into equal parts called "minutes". The i-th of the shows is going from l_i-th to r_i-th minute, both ends inclusive.

You need a TV to watch a TV show and you can't watch two TV shows which air at the same time on the same TV, so it is possible you will need multiple TVs in some minutes. For example, if segments [l_i, r_i] and [l_j, r_j] intersect, then shows i and j can't be watched simultaneously on one TV.

Once you start watching a show on some TV it is not possible to "move" it to another TV (since it would be too distracting), or to watch another show on the same TV until this show ends.

There is a TV Rental shop near you. It rents a TV for x rupees, and charges y (y < x) rupees for every extra minute you keep the TV. So in order to rent a TV for minutes [a; b] you will need to pay x + y ⋅ (b - a). 

You can assume, that taking and returning of the TV doesn't take any time and doesn't distract from watching other TV shows. Find the minimum possible cost to view all shows. Since this value could be too large, print it modulo 10^9 + 7.

Input

The first line contains integers n, x and y (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5, 1 ≤ y < x ≤ 10^9) — the number of TV shows, the cost to rent a TV for the first minute and the cost to rent a TV for every subsequent minute.

Each of the next n lines contains two integers l_i and r_i (1 ≤ l_i ≤ r_i ≤ 10^9) denoting the start and the end minute of the i-th TV show.

Output

Print exactly one integer — the minimum cost to view all the shows taken modulo 10^9 + 7.

Examples

Input

5 4 3
1 2
4 10
2 4
10 11
5 9


Output

60

Input

6 3 2
8 20
6 22
4 15
20 28
17 25
20 27


Output

142

Input

2 1000000000 2
1 2
2 3


Output

999999997

Note

In the first example, the optimal strategy would be to rent 3 TVs to watch:

  * Show [1, 2] on the first TV,
  * Show [4, 10] on the second TV,
  * Shows [2, 4], [5, 9], [10, 11] on the third TV. 



This way the cost for the first TV is 4 + 3 ⋅ (2 - 1) = 7, for the second is 4 + 3 ⋅ (10 - 4) = 22 and for the third is 4 + 3 ⋅ (11 - 2) = 31, which gives 60 int total.

In the second example, it is optimal watch each show on a new TV.

In third example, it is optimal to watch both shows on a new TV. 1. We have n TV shows, each airing from minute `l_i` to minute `r_i` (inclusive)

3. This is essentially a "minimum interval covering" problem where we want to group non-overlapping shows to minimize the sum of interval lengths
